Decisions span December 1999 to February 2026.

B. What Tribunals Decide: The Coded Sample

Of the 160 coded decisions, 109 were disciplinary. Weighted to the corpus, breaches of professional conduct were the most common misconduct type, found in 68.7% of disciplinary decisions (95% CI 58.9-77.5), followed by dishonesty or fraud (41.6%; 29.7-53.1), clinical competence failures (38.6%; 27.5-50.3), and boundary violations (36.3%; 24.8-48.0) (Table 2; Figure 2; Supplementary Table S27). Prescribing misconduct was found in 24.7% (14.9-35.3), impairment in 7.5% (2.3-14.2), and communication failures in 5.5% (0.6-12.0). Among the 39 coded boundary-violation decisions, the weighted shares involving sexual, emotional, and financial breaches were 83.6%, 76.4%, and 5.5% (on the emotional share, see the reliability results below).

Professional misconduct, the most serious finding, was made in 83.0% of disciplinary decisions (74.7-90.4); unsatisfactory professional conduct in 10.0%, unprofessional conduct in 3.0%, and no finding in 4.0%. Reprimand was the most common order (70.9%; 61.0-79.9), followed by conditions on registration (40.0%), cancellation of registration (30.6%; 19.8-42.4), and suspension (20.8%); fines were ordered in 6.6%, and non-standard orders such as costs, disqualification, or prohibition in 39.2%. Reprimand usually accompanied another order rather than standing alone. Medical practitioners were the subject of 41.7% of disciplinary decisions, nurses and midwives of 29.7%, and other professions of 28.7%.

Neither added check changed the picture: the classifier's prevalence in the 600 excluded decisions was within 6 percentage points of its prevalence in the pool for every misconduct type, and re-weighting so that each practitioner counted about once moved each type by 2.2 percentage points or less, and reprimand from 70.9% to 77.5% (Supplementary Table S32).

Agreement with the independent second coder on the 60-decision subset was substantial or better for most fields (Supplementary Table S33). Kappa was 0.79 (95% CI 0.60-0.95) for disciplinary status and 0.76 for the finding, and between 0.84 and 0.96 for profession, boundary violations, prescribing misconduct, and the main orders. It was moderate for dishonesty or fraud (0.59; 0.33-0.82) and professional conduct (0.60; 0.39-0.80), and lower for non-standard orders (0.37); communication failures were too rare for kappa. On the subset (unweighted, so these shares differ from the weighted estimates above), the second coder found professional conduct in 37.0% of the decisions they coded as disciplinary, against 58.5% for the author (difference -21.6 percentage points; 95% CI -36.6 to -6.6); no other misconduct type differed by more than 10.3 points (Supplementary Table S34). Of the 90 field-level disagreements, 81 were definitional or concerned the scope of the decision, and only 9 reflected different readings of the text (Supplementary Table S35). All five disagreements on disciplinary status concerned procedural, review, interim, costs, or withdrawn-complaint decisions that the author coded as non-disciplinary. Among the 14 decisions both coders classed as boundary violations, the author recorded an emotional breach in 11 and the second coder in 1. Note: Coded-sample estimates are weighted to the sampling pool (weight = pool size of the period / 40), with 95% confidence intervals from 10,000 bootstrap resamples within periods. Misconduct types, outcomes, and findings are shares of disciplinary decisions; misconduct types and outcomes are multi-label, so they do not sum to 100%. The classifier column is the share of the 2,477 decisions the classifier labelled disciplinary (exploratory).

C. How Well Automated Labels Reproduce the Coding

On the 45 gold-standard test decisions, the classifier's misconduct macro-F1 was 0.72 (Table 3; by period in Supplementary Table S30). On the coded sample it was 0.70 (95% CI 0.61-0.77), with disciplinary accuracy 0.98 (0.94-1.00), finding accuracy 0.89, and outcome micro-F1 0.74. Performance differed widely between categories. Boundary violations (F1 0.88) and prescribing misconduct (0.83) were classified well. Professional conduct (0.76) and clinical competence (0.70) were classified with high precision (0.89 and 0.84) but missed about a third and two-fifths of true cases (recall 0.66 and 0.59). Dishonesty or fraud had high precision (0.83) but low recall (0.54), so almost half of coded fraud matters were missed. Impairment (0.54) and communication failures (0.24, precision 0.21, on 5 coded cases) were classified poorly.

These error patterns explain the gap between the two sets of estimates in Figure 2. The classifier put dishonesty or fraud at 22.6% of disciplinary decisions against 41.6% in the coded sample, and professional conduct at 52.2% against 68.7%; it put communication failures at 13.7% against 5.5%. Its estimates for clinical competence (36.8%), boundary violations (29.0%), and prescribing (29.2%) fell within the coded sample's confidence intervals. Regex outcome extraction put cancellation at 39.7% of disciplinary decisions against 30.6% in the coded sample, consistent with its tendency to over-detect cancellation on the first test set. Rogan-Gladen adjustment is unstable with sensitivity as low as 0.29 and is given only as a sensitivity analysis (Supplementary Table S28).

Classifier error also changed over time for one category. Among coded fraud matters, the odds that the classifier detected the matter rose 2.83-fold per decade (95% CI 1.11-7.25). For the other categories with at least 10 coded cases, neither detection nor false positives showed clear dependence on year (Supplementary Table S29). Because communication failures had only 5 coded cases, their error could not be checked over time.

The logged language-model coder was run once on the same 160 decisions, with no failed or refused items. Its weighted macro-F1 was 0.77 (0.69-0.83), with disciplinary accuracy 0.96, finding accuracy 0.93, and outcome micro-F1 0.94. It was much better than the classifier on dishonesty or fraud (F1 0.85), and its detection of fraud did not change with year (odds ratio 0.64 per decade, 0.12-3.35). Its advantage over the classifier in macro-F1 was 0.07, with a 95% confidence interval of -0.01 to 0.15. It therefore met the accuracy thresholds but not the requirement that its advantage be clearly greater than zero, and under the protocol the coded sample became the primary evidence on prevalence.

D. Change over Time

In the coded sample, the share of disciplinary decisions involving breaches of professional conduct rose from 57.1% in 1999-2009 to 84.4% in 2020-2026 (odds ratio 2.08 per decade, 95% CI 1.16-3.75, p = 0.014; Table 4). Clinical competence failures fell from 57.1% to 31.2% (odds ratio 0.56 per decade, 0.32-1.00, p = 0.051). Neither change is significant after correction across the seven misconduct types (q = 0.10 and 0.18), so both are best read alongside the corpus-wide trends below, which run in the same directions. Dishonesty or fraud was common at both ends of the period (42.9% in 1999-2009 and 50.0% in 2020-2026, with 13.8% in 2010-2014) and showed no clear trend (odds ratio 1.42, 0.79-2.56). There was no evidence of change for boundary violations (0.97, 0.54-1.72), prescribing (0.79, 0.43-1.46), impairment (0.49, 0.20-1.18), or communication failures, but with 20 to 32 disciplinary decisions per period these comparisons could detect only large changes. Note: Percentages are unweighted shares of the disciplinary decisions coded in each period (N per period: 28, 29, 20, and 32). Odds ratios are from unweighted logistic regression on decision year; q is the Benjamini-Hochberg adjusted p-value across the seven misconduct types.

The number of disciplinary decisions increased strongly across complete years from 1999 to 2025 (Mann-Kendall tau = 0.877, FDR-adjusted p < 0.001; Sen's slope 10.5 decisions per year). This growth outpaced the registered workforce (Figure 3; Table 5). Across the ten professions regulated since 2010, the rate rose from 1.25 disciplinary decisions per 10,000 registrants in 2011-2013 to 2.36 in 2023-2025, an increase of about 5% a year (rate ratio 1.050 per year, 95% CI 1.029-1.071). Rates rose for medical practitioners (4.54 to 6.34 per 10,000; rate ratio 1.027, 1.004-1.051) and most steeply for nurses and midwives (0.39 to 1.38; 1.100, 1.063-1.137). Chiropractic also rose (rate ratio 1.162, 1.066-1.268), but on only 38 decisions. Rates for pharmacy, psychology, and dental practitioners showed no clear trend. The all-profession result was unchanged when the pandemic sub-register was included (rate ratio 1.049) and stronger when decisions of unknown profession were allocated (1.066) (Supplementary Table S31). 5 but not plotted) Note: Rates are disciplinary decisions per 10,000 registrants at 30 June of each year (Ahpra annual reports), including non-practising registrants and excluding the temporary pandemic sub-register. The allprofession row uses the ten professions regulated since July 2010. Decisions of unknown profession are excluded. Chiropractic rests on 38 decisions. The classifier's annual misconduct-type proportions (Supplementary Figure S15) showed significant increases for dishonesty or fraud (tau = 0.462, FDR-adjusted p = 0.001) and communication failures (tau = 0.590, p < 0.001), and no significant trend for the other categories over the full period. Excluding the years before 2005, when annual counts were small, professional conduct increased (tau = 0.394) and clinical competence decreased (tau = -0.528) (Supplementary Table S24), the same directions as in the coded sample. Neither full-period trend can be called robust under the pre-specified check. For dishonesty or fraud, coded and predicted prevalence moved in the same direction, but the classifier detected 25% of coded fraud matters in each of the two earliest periods and 56% to 57% in the two latest (Figure 4), so part of its rising trend reflects changing error. The communication-failure trend could not be checked (5 coded cases) and rests on a category the classifier labels poorly, so it is not interpretable. A three-period comparison around the COVID-19 pandemic showed shifts in five of seven categories that mirror these longerterm changes and cannot be separated from them (Supplementary Table S11).

E. Exploratory Corpus-Wide Comparisons

The analyses in this section use the automated labels for all 2,477 disciplinary decisions and are exploratory; Table 3 shows how far each can be trusted. Co-occurrence of misconduct types is reported in Supplementary Tables S21 to S23.

Cancellation varied by profession (chi-square = 106.25, df = 9, FDR-adjusted p < 0.001, Cramér's V = 0.209); among professions with more than 100 disciplinary decisions it was highest for pharmacists (55.6%) and lowest for medical practitioners (35.2%), although outcome extraction over-detects cancellation (Table 6). Boundary violations were labelled in 74.4% of psychologist decisions, consistent with Kremer's finding for psychologists. 10 The most severe outcome varied by jurisdiction (chi-square = 545.01, df = 56, FDR-adjusted p < 0.001, V = 0.177), most strongly between Queensland and both New South Wales (V = 0.491) and Victoria (V = 0.440); six of seven misconduct types also varied by jurisdiction, but boundary violations did not (V = 0.058, FDR-adjusted p = 0.329) (Supplementary Tables S9 and S10). 7). Mentions include incidental references, so these are upper bounds. Opioid-related prescribing decisions were more often labelled as ending in cancellation than other prescribing decisions (42.0% vs 32.3%; FDR-adjusted p = 0.018; Supplementary Table S5). For boundary violations, also labelled well (F1 0.88), the keyword subcategories were accurate for sexual breaches against the coded sample (precision and recall 0.97) but not for financial breaches (precision 0.21), so the coded sample's 5.5% is the better financial estimate (Supplementary Table S6).

IV. Discussion

IV. Discussion

Principal Findings

This study gives the first estimates of the make-up of Australian health practitioner tribunal discipline that are representative of the published decisions across all professions, jurisdictions, and a 27-year period. Breaches of professional conduct, a broad category that includes breaches of registration conditions and conduct outside practice, were found in more than two-thirds of disciplinary decisions in the author's coding (an independent coder applied this residual category less often), and dishonesty or fraud in about two in five; clinical competence failures and boundary violations each featured in more than a third. Professional misconduct, the most serious finding, was made in 83% of disciplinary decisions, consistent with the referral threshold that directs the most serious matters to tribunals. Professional conduct breaches appear to have become more common over time, and clinical competence failures less common, although neither change is certain. Disciplinary decisions grew faster than the registered workforce, roughly doubling per registrant between 2011-2013 and 2023-2025. Automated labels understated dishonesty and professional conduct, and for dishonesty their error changed over time.

Dishonesty and Professional Conduct

The prominence of dishonesty is the largest difference between the coded sample and the automated labels: the classifier would have put fraud fifth among seven categories at 23%, where the coded sample puts it second at 42%. Dishonesty in these decisions covers false records, misleading statements to employers or regulators, misrepresented qualifications, and fraudulent billing. It matters for outcome as well as frequency: decision-makers in UK healthcare regulation regard dishonesty, like sexual misconduct, as less remediable than clinical failings, 38 so its share bears directly on how often removal from practice is likely. The independent second coder found dishonesty slightly more often than the author on the reliability subset (37.0% against 29.3% of disciplinary decisions, a difference within chance), so its prominence does not rest on the author's coding alone. Dishonesty was common in both the earliest and the latest periods of the coded sample, and the sample shows no clear trend. The classifier's rising fraud trend is at least partly an artefact of the classifier's improving detection of fraud in later decisions.

The rise in professional conduct breaches is better supported than the fraud trend but not established: their share in the coded sample rose from 57% of disciplinary decisions before 2010 to 84% from 2020, a change not significant once seven misconduct types are tested, and the automated labels agree in direction once the sparse early years are excluded. The rise may reflect more matters about compliance with earlier regulatory action and about conduct beyond clinical care, as well as changes in how such conduct is charged; the 2022 paramount principle commenced too late in the corpus to account for it. 6 Professional conduct is also the category on which the two coders agreed least. On the reliability subset the second coder found it in 37% of disciplinary decisions rather than 59%. In 6 of the 9 decisions where only the author coded it, the conduct was dishonest, and the author coded it as professional conduct as well as fraud. As a broad residual category, its prevalence depends on how widely it is applied, and the 68.7% estimate reflects the author's reading of the guidelines.

Clinical Competence and Impairment

Clinical competence failures fell from more than half of disciplinary decisions before 2015 to under a third from 2020, although the evidence is at the margin of statistical significance. More clinical performance concerns may now be managed by National Boards through conditions or performance assessment; the 2022 discretion not to refer matters to a tribunal is an unlikely explanation, since the 2025 review found it had not significantly affected referrals. 8 Impairment featured in 7.5% of disciplinary decisions. About 1% of doctors outside New South Wales were the subject of a health impairment notification over a decade, 39 but the National Law's health assessment and Board action pathways may keep most such concerns from tribunals, 40 and the 2020 narrowing of treating practitioners' mandatory notification duties for practitioners' own health may have reduced them further. 41

Boundary Violations

Boundary violations featured in more than a third of disciplinary decisions, and sexual breaches in 84% of these. This accords with Kremer's finding that boundary violations dominate psychologist decisions, 10 and with Millbank's finding that sexual misconduct made up one in five serious tribunal matters under the National Law and attracted the most severe outcomes. 42 Upstream, sexual misconduct notifications concerned a small proportion of practitioners (0.2% over six years) but led to sanctions more often than other notifications. 43 There was no evidence that the share of boundary violations changed over time, either in the coded sample or in the automated labels, despite the introduction of mandatory notification in 2010 and the Medical Board's sexual boundary guidelines (2011, revised 2018). 44, 45 Because these are shares of tribunal decisions, an increase in the number of boundary matters could be masked by faster growth in other categories. In the author's coding, emotional boundary breaches accompanied most sexual ones, consistent with the 'slippery slope' account, in which sexual exploitation of patients often follows a series of earlier non-sexual boundary crossings. 46 The independent second coder, however, recorded an emotional breach in only 1 of the 14 boundary decisions both coders identified, against 11 for the author. The emotional share therefore depends on how broadly the subtype is read and should be treated with caution.

In the exploratory corpus-wide analysis, decisions labelled as boundary violations were not significantly more likely to end in cancellation than other disciplinary decisions (42.0% vs 38.7%; FDR-adjusted p = 0.164; Supplementary Table S7), unlike Millbank's finding that proved sexual misconduct attracted the highest rate of removal from practice of any head of misconduct; 47 but the boundary category here includes non-sexual breaches, the comparison decisions also often end in cancellation, and the outcome labels over-detect cancellation. Since April 2026, a finding of professional misconduct with a sexual-misconduct basis is recorded permanently on the public register, whatever the sanction and retrospectively to decisions made from July 2010 (the first round of publication covered 107 practitioners). 7 A non-cancelling outcome in a sexual misconduct matter therefore no longer leaves a lighter public record, which may alter how such matters are charged and contested, and future analyses will need to treat 2026 as a break.

Prescribing Misconduct

Prescribing misconduct featured in about a quarter of disciplinary decisions, and almost two-thirds of the decisions labelled as prescribing misconduct mentioned an opioid, which bears on Australia's efforts to reduce pharmaceutical opioid-related harm 48 and parallels licensing-board discipline for opioid prescribing in the United States. 49 Benzodiazepines were also prominent, consistent with their contribution to overdose deaths in Australia and the rescheduling of alprazolam to Schedule 8 in 2014. 50 The share of prescribing misconduct showed no evidence of change over time, in either the coded sample or the automated la-bels, despite real-time prescription monitoring (SafeScript in Victoria and the national system) and the rescheduling of codeine to prescription-only status in 2018. 51, 52, 53

Growth Relative to the Workforce

Across the ten professions regulated since 2010, the rate of disciplinary decisions per registrant roughly doubled between 2011-2013 and 2023-2025, and it rose more than threefold for nurses and midwives. The growth may reflect more notifications reaching tribunals, more complete publication of decisions over time, more procedural and appeal decisions per matter, or changes in practitioner conduct, and counts of decisions cannot tell these apart; the denominator also includes non-practising registrants (about 2% to 3% of the total). Whatever its cause, it has regulatory consequences: the 2025 review reported that average investigation times rose by about half between 2018-19 and 2022-23.8

Jurisdictional Variation

The exploratory analysis found large differences in outcomes between jurisdictions, most strongly between Queensland and both New South Wales and Victoria. They may reflect co-regulatory structures (New South Wales has a separate Health Care Complaints Commission, and Queensland a Health Ombudsman), 54 the mix of matters referred, tribunal composition and practice, and pre-National Law frameworks, as well as genuine differences in how similar cases are treated; separating them would require coding of case severity. The 2025 review recommended longitudinal research to establish whether materially similar cases are treated differently between jurisdictions, and Health Ministers have asked Ahpra to research outcomes for 2020 to 2025. 8 A combined design like this one, with automated labels for the corpus, representative human coding to measure their error, and coding of case severity, could support that work.

What Automated Analysis Can and Cannot Show

The study also shows how automated analysis of a legal corpus can go wrong in ways a conventional test set does not reveal. The classifier performed almost the same on its original test set and on the representative sample (macro-F1 0.72 and 0.70), and it identified disciplinary decisions accurately. Yet its corpuswide estimates understated the second most common misconduct type by almost half, overstated a rare one more than twofold, and produced a rising trend that is partly a product of its own changing error. None of these problems is visible in an overall F1 score. The mechanisms are known in principle: errors in automated labels bias the statistics computed from them even when a classifier scores well on F1, 55 and a classifier's error changes when the data it labels differ from the data it was trained on, as decisions written in different periods do. 56 What this study adds is the size of those effects in a real regulatory corpus, and a practical check that exposes them. The problems become visible only when automated labels are compared with human coding on a sample that represents the corpus and spans its time period. Studies that use machine-learning or language-model labels to describe legal or regulatory corpora, including recent work on health-related court decisions and complaint reports, 19 should report category-level error on a representative sample and test whether error changes over time before reporting trends. The point is not specific to Australia. Disciplinary actions by United States licensing boards are public records that researchers already review case by case, for example to study discipline for opioid prescribing, 49 and recent guidance on automated methods in legal epidemiology identifies validation as a central challenge. 21 A representative, period-stratified validation sample is a practical way to meet it. The human reference needs its own check. Here an independent coder agreed closely with the author on disciplinary status, boundary violations, and prescribing, but only moderately on dishonesty and professional conduct, so the classifier's error for those two categories is measured against a less certain standard.

A language model, used as a coder with a fixed and logged prompt, performed better than the classifier, particularly for dishonesty (F1 0.85 against 0.66), and its detection of fraud did not drift. Its advantage was not large enough, on 160 decisions, to meet the pre-specified threshold for replacing human coding, and agreement with one author's coding measures fidelity to the coding guidelines rather than legal correctness. Language-model coders should face the same test as classifiers: category-level error on a representative sample, checked across time.

Limitations

The main threat to the conclusions is the size and coding of the representative sample. The primary estimates come from 160 decisions coded by one person, so the confidence intervals are wide, rare categories (impairment and communication failures) are imprecise, and only large changes over time can be detected.

An independent second coder recoded 60 of the 160 decisions. Agreement was good for disciplinary status, the finding, profession, the main orders, and most misconduct types. It was lower for dishonesty or fraud and professional conduct, the categories that turn most on judgement about breadth, and the two coders applied the emotional boundary subtype very differently. The estimates for professional conduct and for the boundary subtypes therefore depend partly on the author's interpretation of the guidelines. The second coder spent a median of under two minutes per decision, below the four minutes required at screening. In an exploratory comparison, however, agreement was higher in the faster second session than in the first (Supplementary Appendix H), so the pace does not appear to have degraded the codes. Most disagreements were definitional or about the scope of a decision, which points to rules that could be written more precisely, for example on procedural decisions, where the guidelines' edge-case rule and the field rule differ; the author applied the field rule throughout, so the primary estimates use one definition. The estimates describe the sampling pool, which excludes the 600 decisions used for the gold standard and for training; those were drawn by profession and jurisdiction, so their exclusion is unlikely to bias the estimates materially.

The sample was also coded twice, and the recode, a departure from the protocol, was made with knowledge of the aggregate results of the first scoring, although without sight of any individual prediction (Supplementary Appendix G).

The corpus-wide analyses are exploratory and, apart from the counts and rates, rest on automated labels with the error rates in Table 3. Associations between groups may be biased by error that differs between them (the classifier's error by profession on the first test set varied widely, and its error by jurisdiction has not been measured), and outcome extraction over-detects cancellation. The gold standard was checked against the same language model that labelled 300 of the 510 training decisions, so training labels and the first test reference partly share a source, but performance against the author's blind codes alone was essentially unchanged (macro-F1 0.721 against 0.719; Supplementary Table S26), and the coded sample has no model input.

The corpus may be incomplete. Six legacy state tribunals were unrecoverable, smaller jurisdictions are underrepresented, and the corpus includes only published decisions. Matters resolved by consent, surrender of registration, or other non-adjudicated pathways are not included, and tribunals may publish selectively. The findings describe published tribunal decisions, the most serious end of regulatory action, and cannot be generalised to all practitioner misconduct. The unit of analysis is the decision, not the matter: one matter may produce several decisions (for example, liability, sanction, and appeal), which affects comparison with case-based studies such as Millbank's.

Rates per registrant use annual registrant counts that include non-practising registrants, and depend on the profession recorded in each decision, which was unknown for 311 decisions in 2011-2025. This is a descriptive study, and no causal inference should be drawn from the associations reported.

Future Directions

Three extensions would strengthen this work. Double coding of the whole representative sample, under guidelines revised where this study's coders diverged (procedural decisions, the professional conduct category, and the emotional boundary subtype), would make the primary estimates less dependent on one coder's reading. A larger representative sample, or coding targeted at rare categories, would narrow the intervals and allow change in boundary violations, impairment, and communication failures to be tested. Coding of case severity, representation, and attendance, which Surgenor and colleagues found important in New Zealand, 13 would allow jurisdictional comparisons that address case mix. Corpus-wide monitoring with automated labels, whether from classifiers or language models, is worth pursuing only with periodic re-validation against representative human coding, especially after legal changes such as the 2026 register reforms.

V. Conclusion

Across 3,586 Australian tribunal decisions from December 1999 to February 2026, a representative coded sample shows that tribunal discipline is made up largely of breaches of professional conduct and dishonesty, as well as clinical failings and boundary violations (although the professional conduct share depends on how widely that broad category is applied), that most decisions end in a finding of professional misconduct, and that about one in three ends in cancellation. Professional conduct matters appear to have become more common and clinical competence matters less so, and disciplinary decisions have grown much faster than the registered workforce. Automated classification reproduced some of these findings and distorted others, understating dishonesty and producing a fraud trend partly through its own changing error. Published decisions can supply the research on tribunal outcomes over time that the 2025 review asked for, provided that automated labels are checked against representative human coding across the whole period studied.

AI Use Disclosure

Claude Sonnet 5.5 (Anthropic; model identifier claude-sonnet-5-5) was used in three logged batch runs with the same fixed prompt: as the second coder of the 300 gold-standard decisions (Methods section B), to label 300 further decisions used as supplementary training data (Methods section D), and once on the 160-decision coded sample to test whether it should replace the classifier (Methods section E). Every request and response was logged, and no model output was edited. All human coding was done by the author, blind to the model's codes, except the 60-decision reliability subset, which was also coded by an independent paid coder who attested to using no AI tools; every disagreement between the author and the model in the gold standard was adjudicated by the author; the coded sample has no model input. Claude Code (Anthropic) assisted with code development, reference checking, data checks (including the checks that identified the misaligned first coding of the coded sample), and manuscript drafting and editing. It also staged the second coder's Prolific studies and workbooks, froze the returned files, computed agreement from them, and drafted the descriptive classification of the coders' disagreements by type (Supplementary Table S35), which the author reviewed; it did not create or change any coder's codes. No Claude Code output was used as data or as a reference standard. The classifiers themselves are TF-IDF logistic regression models, not language models. The author decided the study design, the author's coding of every decision, and every adjudication, reviewed and verified all content, and takes responsibility for it.

Ethics Statement

This study analysed publicly available legal decisions published by Australian tribunals. No ethics approval was required. Results are reported in aggregate, and no practitioner is named. The independent second coder was engaged as a paid contributor providing a coding service, not as a research participant; no personal data were collected beyond a pseudonymous platform identifier, which is not reported, and the coder agreed to be acknowledged by initials. Precision is the share of a classifier's labels for a misconduct type that the human coder also gave; recall (sensitivity) is the share of the coder's labels that the classifier also found.
